To visualize the aero package that achieved the "Perfect Simulation," look
for these speciﬁc components in the design. They transform the 206 from
a standard city car into a high-speed stable platorm:
1. The Front Spliter: This is a ﬂat, horizontal extension at the very
botom of the front bumper. Its job is to separate the air, forcing
"high-pressure" air over the top of the car while creating a "low-
pressure" vacuum underneath.
2. WRC-Spec Roof Wing: Unlike a simple lip spoiler, this sits at the top of
the rear hatch. It is angled to "clean up" the air coming oﬀ the roof,
providing downforce that keeps the rear wheels planted at speeds
over 180 km/h.
3. The Rear Diﬀuser: Located under the rear bumper, these vertical ﬁns
help the air under the car expand and slow down smoothly. This
reduces the "drag" that usually pulls back on hatchbacks.
Technical Placement Note for the Encyclopedia:
Spliter Material: 10mm Marine Plywood (wrapped in
Carbon/Fiberglass) or High-Density Polyethylene (HDPE). It must be
strong enough to stand on, as it will be under hundreds of kilograms of
air pressure at V-Max.

Chassis Mounting: These parts should be bolted directly to the
Chassis or Subframe, not just the plastic bumper. If you bolt the
spliter only to the plastic, the air pressure will rip the bumper oﬀ the
car at 200 km/h.
Is there a speciﬁc section of the aero—like the under-tray or the brake
ducts—you want to see in a more technical "X-Ray" view?
How to minimalize aero to keep the sleeper look and still
manage to fix problems?
To keep the "Sleeper" look of a 5-door hatchback while maintaining the
high-speed stability and cooling of our "Perfect Simulation," we must move
all the aerodynamic work underneath the car.
In the tuning world, this is known as "Hidden Aero." You want the car to
look like a standard, well-kept Peugeot 206, while the chassis works like a
race car.
1. The Hidden Front "Air Dam"
Instead of a large, visible spliter, we use a Vertical Air Dam.
The Look: Use a simple black rubber lip (like the one from a Seat Leon
Cupra R or a Volvo 860). It is ﬂexible, subtle, and looks like a factory
plastic trim.
The Function: It blocks air from going under the car's messy
components (oil pan, subframe) and forces it through the
radiator/intercooler instead. This reduces front-end lif signiﬁcantly
without the "race car" look.
2. Full Underbody Paneling (The Secret Weapon)
The biggest "sleeper" upgrade is a Flat Floor
https://gemini.google.com/share/fea11402ece6?social_preview=false&skid=e6031dbd-6829-4c40-bef6-76a3cda83769
10/19/26, 2:02 AM
Page 223 of 292

The biggest sleeper upgrade is a Flat Floor.
The Hardware: Use 2mm Aluminum or ABS plastic sheets to cover the
entire underside of the car—from the front bumper to the rear axle.
Why it works: It turns the "dirty" air under the car into a smooth, fast-
moving stream. This creates a vacuum that "sucks" the car to the road
at high speeds.
Note: You must leave a "window" or ducting for the exhaust system to
prevent heat from being trapped against the ﬂoorboards.
3. Passive "Stealth" Venting
The simulation showed that trapped air under the hood causes lif. To ﬁx
this without cuting giant holes in your hood:
Wheel Arch Liners: Cut "louvers" (slots) into the plastic inner wheel
well liners behind the front wheels. This allows high-pressure air
trapped in the engine bay to vent into the low-pressure zone of the
wheel arches.
The "Cowl" Trick: Remove the rubber seal at the back of the engine
bay where the hood meets the windshield. It’s invisible from the
outside but allows a massive amount of hot air to escape.
4. Subtle Rear Stability (The "GTI" Spoiler)
The Look: Instead of the giant WRC wing, use the OEM Peugeot 206
RC/GTI 180 spoiler.
The Function: It is small and looks factory-original, but it is speciﬁcally
designed to clean up the air "detaching" from the roof. This stops the
rear of the car from feeling "ﬂoaty" at 200 km/h.

To achieve the "Perfect Middle Ground," we are moving away from the
slammed "Track" setup and toward what professionals call a Tarmac Rally /
Safari Spec.
This setup keeps the car looking like a clean "Sleeper" on the street but
gives it the mechanical "travel" and protection needed to survive high-
speed rough terrain.
1. The Suspension: "Safari-Street" Coilovers
Instead of stiﬀ, short-stroke race springs, we use Long-Travel Adjustable
Coilovers (like the Gaz Gold Tarmac/Gravel hybrid or Bilstein B6).
Ride Height: Set to +10mm to +15mm higher than a standard 206 GTI.
This looks like a "healthy" stock height (Sleeper status), but gives the
suspension room to breathe over bumps.
Spring Rate: Use a Progressive Rate Spring. It’s sof for the ﬁrst 2
inches of travel (comfort on gravel) but ﬁrms up signiﬁcantly under
hard cornering (control on tarmac).
Damping: Set your "Bump" (compression) to Medium-Sof. This allows
the wheels to move up quickly when hiting a rock rather than
slamming the energy into the chassis.
2. The "Hidden Armor" Package
Since we are staying "Sleeper," we can't have a giant rally bash-bar, but we
need the protection.
The Sump Guard: Install a 4mm Steel or 6mm Aluminum Skid Plate.
Unlike the plastic "Sleeper" aero, this is bolted directly to the
subframe. It must have a "leading edge" that curves up into the front
bumper to act as a slide-plate if you botom out.
Brake Line Armor: Wrap your rear brake lines in stainless steel
braiding and plastic spiral wrap. On rough terrain, small stones can
act like bullets; one lucky hit can cut a standard rubber line.
Mud Flaps (The Stealth Version): Use Black Polyurethane Flaps

Mud Flaps (The Stealth Version): Use Black Polyurethane Flaps
trimmed to be only 1 inch wider than the tire. They are almost invisible

3. The "Hybrid" Wheels & Tires
This is where the middle ground is won or lost.
The Wheels: Stick with your Black Multi-spokes, but ensure they are

secondary shock absorber.

the Michelin Pilot Sport All Season 4 or Falken Wildpeak A/T Trail if they
ﬁt). You want a tire with a reinforced sidewall that won't "pinch-ﬂat"
when you hit a pothole at 100 km/h.

the Haldex rear unit. This ensures a ﬂying rock doesn't crack the alloy

This is the Black Ops of suspension tuning. To get 30% oﬀroad
capability without the high-center of gravity (body roll) or the "lifed" look,
we have to use high-frequency damping and mechanical limiters.
Instead of making the car taller, we make the suspension smarter and the
chassis stiﬀer.
1. The "Dual-Piston" Suspension (The Secret to No Roll)
To keep the car low but capable of hiting a jump or a rock, we use
Frequency Selective Damping (FSD) or high-end inverted dampers with
Internal Bump Stops.
The Tuning: We keep the car at Factory RC/GTI height (or just
).
How it works: On smooth tarmac, the valving stays stiﬀ, keeping the
car ﬂat in corners (zero roll). When you hit a high-speed bump oﬀ-
road, the "High-Speed Circuit" in the shock opens up instantly,
absorbing the impact so the car doesn't "jump" or botom out.
The Part: Bilstein B8 (shorter, stiﬀer) paired with progressive-rate
Eibach springs.
2. Anti-Roll Bar (ARB) Optimization
To prevent the "ﬂoppiness" of oﬀ-road setups while keeping the tires on
the ground:
Front: Upgrade to a thicker solid ARB (from a 206 SW or Diesel model
—they are ofen thicker to handle engine weight). This keeps the front
ﬂat.
Rear: Since you have the AWD Subframe, we use a stiﬀ rear sway bar.
The Result: The car corner-carves like a go-kart on the street, but the

You’ve hit the nail on the head. That "Pro-Driver" spec is exactly where the
bill starts to look like a supercar's maintenance invoice. When you try to
make a car do everything perfectly (high speed, no roll, and oﬀ-road
durability), you end up buying parts that are basically custom-engineered.
To bring Project Snow White 2.0 back to earth without losing the "Sleeper"
soul or the AWD power, we need to do the "Smart Builder" Budget Trim.
Here is how we keep the performance but cut the "High-End" costs by
about 60%.
1. The "Poor Man's" Smart Suspension
Instead of FSD or custom inverted dampers:
The Part: Peugeot 206 RC (GTI 180) OEM Dampers.

The Part: Peugeot 206 RC (GTI 180) OEM Dampers.
The Hack: These were designed by Peugeot’s rally engineers for the
road. They are stiﬀer than standard 206 parts but incredibly durable.
Pair them with a standard set of Eibach lowering springs (
).
Oﬀ-Road Capability: You lose the "jump-ability," but the RC dampers
are "valved" well enough to handle a fast dirt road without exploding.
Savings: Saves you roughly $1,200 over the "Pro" setup.
2. The "DIY" Armor
Instead of Titanium or Hardox steel:
The Part: Heavy-duty HDPE Plastic (10mm thick).
The Hack: Go to an industrial plastic supplier and buy a sheet of High-
Density Polyethylene. You can cut it with a jigsaw and bend it with a
heat gun.
The Result: It won't stop a 100mph rock from denting the subframe,
but it will slide over gravel and protect your oil pan from 95% of
threats. It’s also completely silent (steel plates ratle and hum).
Savings: Saves you $400.
3. The "Standard Multi-Spoke" Wheel Choice
Instead of high-end forged wheels or "SUV" speciﬁc performance tires:
The Part: Used 17" OEM wheels from a later model Peugeot or Ford
(same 4x108 bolt patern) and Mid-tier Summer Tires (like Hankook
Ventus).
The Hack: Mid-tier tires have slightly thicker sidewalls than "Ultra-High
Performance" track tires anyway. They handle the 30% oﬀ-road beter
because they aren't as "britle."
Savings: Saves you $800 - $1,000.
4. The Interior "Keep it Simple" Rule
The Part: Keep your Stock Seats and Stock Dash

4. The "Haldex Muscle" Power Wiring
The Haldex pump pulls signiﬁcant current. Do not wire this through the
factory Peugeot fuse box.
1. Main Power: Run a 10AWG Red Wire from the batery to a 30A Relay
in the trunk.
2. Ground: 10AWG Black Wire to a clean chassis point (scrape away the
white paint for a metal-to-metal contact).
3. Trigger: Connect the Relay Trigger to the Haldex Controller’s output.
5. The "Glovebox" Diagnostic Port
Mount the MaxxECU USB Cable inside the glovebox.
To a passenger, it’s just a charging cable.
To you, it’s the port where you plug in your laptop or tablet to see the
F1-level data we simulated.

I think 30% off road is alot we can change tha to 20%
Moving to an 80/20 split (80% Street / 20% Oﬀ-Road) is the "Sweet Spot."
This adjustment allows us to lower the car signiﬁcantly, bringing it back to
that aggressive, level "Sleeper" stance without sacriﬁcing the ability to
survive the occasional trail or dirt shortcut.
At 20% oﬀ-road, we assume the terrain is mostly maintained gravel and
dirt roads rather than ruted forest tracks.
1. The "Precision" Ride Height
The Seting: We drop the height to +5mm to +10mm over stock.
The Visual: This is almost indistinguishable from a standard Peugeot
206 GTI. To the average person, it looks like a perfectly stock
commuter.
The Physics: By lowering the center of gravity compared to the 30%
spec, we drastically reduce Body Roll. This allows the car to corner ﬂat
on tarmac while still having enough "daylight" under the chassis to
avoid scraping on gravel.
2. Suspension: High-Frequency Damping
Since we have less travel to work with, we need the suspension to be
"faster."
The Setup: Bilstein B8 (shorter stroke) with Eibach Pro-Kit springs.
The Logic: The B8 dampers are designed for lowered cars. They are
very stiﬀ on the street (no roll) but have high-quality internal valving
that can soak up a high-speed bump on a dirt road without botoming
out the chassis.
3. The "Light Armor" Strategy
At 20% oﬀroad we can move from heavy steel to lightweight protection
https://gemini.google.com/share/fea11402ece6?social_preview=false&skid=e6031dbd-6829-4c40-bef6-76a3cda83769
10/19/26, 2:02 AM
Page 260 of 292

At 20% oﬀ-road, we can move from heavy steel to lightweight protection.
The Guard: 3mm Aluminum Sump Guard. It’s lighter than the steel
version, helping your 0-100 km/h times, and it’s enough to protect the
oil pan from ﬂying stones and light scrapes.
The Clearance: Because the plate is thinner and the car is lower, we
mount it as close to the engine as possible to maximize ground
clearance.

At 25%, we are building for "B-Road Dominance." This means the car is

ripples) and high-speed unpaved surfaces, while still maintaining a sharp,
aggressive "Sleeper" proﬁle.

Subframe" plate. It must be angled so that if you hit a section of deep
gravel or a "crest" in the road, the car slides over it like a sled rather
than digging in.
The Secret: We add Rubber Isolators between the plate and the
subframe. This keeps the "Sleeper" quietness on the highway by
preventing the metal-on-metal vibration.
3. The "Hybrid" Tire: The "25% Specialist"

best street tire in the world but increase the sidewall height.
The Result: On the street, you have 100% of the grip of a supercar. On
the 25% oﬀ-road sections, the extra sidewall acts as a "buﬀer" to
protect your AWD drivetrain from sharp impacts.

1. The "22.5%" Stance: Precision Geometry
Ride Height: +12mm over stock. * The Logic: At +15mm (25%), the car

Visual: The "Sleeper" eﬀect is at
. To the naked eye, the wheel

2. The "Inverted" Dampening (The Secret Sauce)
To handle 22.5% oﬀ-road without the car feeling "ﬂoaty" on the highway, we
use Inverted Monotube Dampers (like the XYZ Tarmac Rally or BC Racing
DS Series).
How it works: In an inverted strut, the thick part of the shock is at the
top. This makes the suspension much stronger against "side-loading"
(when you're sliding sideways on gravel).
Valving: We set the Low-Speed Compression to be stiﬀ (to kill body
roll on tarmac) but keep the High-Speed Compression blow-oﬀ valve
sof.
Result: The car feels like a go-kart on smooth roads, but the
moment you hit a "square edge" pothole or a rock, the suspension
"blows open" to absorb it instantly.

Why 25% might be "Beter" for you:
If you are even asking "how bad can I go," your brain is already thinking
about the 25% spec.
The "Dip" Factor: When you brake hard on dirt, the front of the car
"dives." With the 22.5% spec, a heavy brake-dive followed by a bump
could botom out your skid plate. The 25% (+15mm) height gives the
suspension more "breathing room" to handle those dynamic weight
shifs.
The AWD Clearance: Remember, you have a rear diﬀerential now. It
hangs lower than the stock torsion beam. That extra 3mm in the 25%
spec ensures the rear diﬀ doesn't "clunk" against a rock during a fast
power-slide.
Why 22.5% is "The Aesthetic King":
If you go with 22.5% (+12mm), the car looks 100% Stock. It is the
ultimate "Sleeper." No one—not even a Peugeot enthusiast—will know
the car is lifed. It looks "tight" and "surgical."
The "Thought Partner" Verdict:
Go with the 25% (+15mm) Oﬀ-Road Spec.
Here is why: You can always use "Spring Sag" to your advantage. A new
25% suspension will naturally "setle" by 2-3mm afer the ﬁrst month of
driving. If you start at 25%, you’ll end up at a perfect 22.5% anyway. If you
start at 22.5%, you might ﬁnd yourself wishing for that extra bit of
clearance the ﬁrst time you hear a rock "ping" oﬀ your skid plate.

Technical Decision: We are locking in the +15mm Ride Height.
This provides the best "High-Speed-Gravel" stability while
keeping the 5-door body looking like a clean, civilized street car.

for eﬃciency. The moment you "ﬂoor it" out of a hair-pin, it locks to
50/50, eliminating front-wheel-drive torque steer.

Can it do euro VI
To give you the most honest "Thought Partner" answer: Technically, yes—
but legally, it is extremely diﬃcult to get on paper.
The EW10J4S engine was originally built for Euro 3 (2003–2005). Asking it
to meet Euro VI (2014+) standards is like asking a professional athlete from
the 90s to pass a modern Olympic drug test; the rules have changed
signiﬁcantly.
Here is the breakdown of how we make Project Snow White 2.0 clean
enough to pass, and why it might not mater.
1. The Technical "Euro VI" Conversion
To hit the strict Euro VI limits (especially the 60mg/km NOx limit for petrol),
we have to upgrade three major systems:
The "Smart" Catalyst: You must replace a standard sport cat with a
Euro 6-rated High-Flow Catalytic Converter (speciﬁcally a "Three-
Way" catalyst with high precious metal loading). This is the single most
important part.
Precision Tuning (The MaxxECU Win): Because we chose the
MaxxECU Sport, we have a massive advantage. We can program a
"City/Emissions Mode" map.
The Logic: At low speeds and idle, the ECU can run a very lean
mixture and use "Target Lambda" control to ensure the exhaust
gas is as clean as possible.
Secondary Air Injection (SAI): Euro VI is very strict about "Cold Start"
emissions. We would need to retain or retroﬁt an electric air pump that
blows fresh air into the exhaust manifold for the ﬁrst 60 seconds of
driving to help the catalyst reach operating temperature instantly.
https://gemini.google.com/share/fea11402ece6?social_preview=false&skid=e6031dbd-6829-4c40-bef6-76a3cda83769
10/19/26, 2:02 AM

2. The Reality: Euro VI vs. ULEZ/Lez
The "Good News": In most cities (like London or Paris), Petrol cars
only need to be Euro 4 to be exempt from daily charges (like ULEZ).
Since your donor engine is Euro 3, a high-quality catalyst and a clean
tune will easily pass a modern tailpipe test (MOT/TÜV), making it
"clean" by most standards.
The "Bad News": Oﬃcially changing the car's logbook from Euro 3 to
Euro VI is almost impossible in many countries. Governments usually
look at the Year of Manufacture, not what you’ve done to the engine.
3. "Sleeper" Reliability & The Budget Impact
Adding Euro VI hardware changes our "Smart Builder" equation:
Heat: Leaner tunes (to reduce CO2) make the engine run hoter. We
would need to upgrade the radiator to a full-aluminum racing core.
Cost: A true Euro VI-rated universal catalyst is roughly $500–$700,
compared to $150 for a standard sport cat.
The "Theory" Recommendation
Don't aim for oﬃcial Euro VI certiﬁcation (it’s a legal nightmare). Instead,
aim for "Technical Compliance."
The Strategy: Build the car with a high-quality Euro 5/6
universal catalyst and have two maps on your MaxxECU:
1. Map A (Interceptor): 375 HP, full performance.
2. Map B (City/Clean): Restricted boost, leaned-out idle,
ultra-low emissions for driving through "Green Zones."
Final Encyclopedia Note: The "Green" Sleeper
Project Snow White 2.0 can be made cleaner than most
